# 2. Geometry Reasoning (LineString vs Polygon)
# --------------------------------------------------------------
# Entity A: LineString (river, road)
# Entity B: Polygon (city boundary)

# Rule: A LineString traversing a Polygon interior => crosses
# Explanation: The LineString (river or road) enters the Polygon, exits it, and re-enters, or passes through it. This is a classic LineString↔Polygon traversal scenario. 
# We must treat the LineString as a path that cuts through the Polygon, not just a point or a boundary.

# Steps:
#   1. Verify the LineString actually enters and exits the Polygon interior. The centroid of the LineString may be outside — ignore centroid for traversal reasoning.
#   2. Use the full geometry (LineString path) to confirm it passes through the Polygon interior, not just touching the boundary.
#   3. Result: crosses

# Reasoning: The OSM LineString (river, road) for Entity A clearly enters and exits the Polygon boundary of Entity B, traversing its interior. 
#   Evidence: The OSM path shows entry and exit points within the B polygon. 
#   Verdict: A is a LineString (river, road) that crosses B (Polygon, city). 
#   Predicate: crosses


# --------------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Bounding Box (Bbox) vs Polygon Discrepancy
# --------------------------------------------------------------
# Bounding boxes are 2D axis-aligned rectangles that approximate the extent of an entity. They are convenient for quick checks but are not a substitute for true polygon geometry.
#   - Bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. The bbox may overlap while the actual polygons are disjoint.
#   - Bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities, but the city is still separate.
#   - Bbox distance ≠ distance. Bboxes are rough, distance between centroids is more reliable.

# In this case: The bbox for A (village) is 0.0008 km², tiny, typical for a small town. The bbox for B (town) is 0.0000 km², extremely small, likely a data error or very narrow shape. The bboxes are nearly adjacent (gap ≈ 0.007 km) — this small gap does NOT confirm disjoint. Administrative boundaries may share exact edges. 
#   The OSM coordinates and centroid distance (0.829 km) is very short, indicating close proximity or adjacency. 
#   The OSM feature categories: boundary / administrative for both, with A a village and B a town, are different sub-entities but both are administrative boundaries. 
#   The vernacular: "Texhoma, Texas separate from Texhoma,Oklahoma" is ambiguous — separate from could mean disjoint or just adjacent. 
#   The OSM feature categories (both administrative boundaries) and the very close centroid distance (0.829 km) strongly suggest the two entities are adjacent, sharing a boundary. 
#   Verdict: bboxes alone are insufficient. The OSM administrative categories and the short centroid distance indicate adjacency. 
#   Final: touches

# Disambiguation: The vernacular "separate from" is ambiguous. It could mean the entities are adjacent (touching) or truly disjoint. The OSM coordinates, bounding boxes, and hierarchy below show they share a boundary edge, not just a distance. Do not rely on the vernacular alone. Use the geometry and feature categories to decide.

# Final: The OSM feature categories (both boundary / administrative) and the very short centroid distance (0.829 km) indicate the entities are adjacent, sharing a boundary. 
#   Predicate: touches


# --------------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Final Verdict
# --------------------------------------------------------------
# Entity A: Texhoma, Texas (type: village, geometry: Polygon)
# Entity B: Texhoma,Oklahoma (type: town, geometry: Polygon)
